Suggested definition:

shepherd-task estate — The complete set of executable scripts and Copilot skills that collectively implement and operate the `shepherd-task` plugin, including lifecycle-stage orchestration, installation, validation, monitoring, inspection, and supporting utilities in both Bash and PowerShell.

There is a manifest, `plugins/shepherd-task/plugin.json`, but it only explicitly composes the six skills:

- `shepherd-task-10-create-ignorance-reduction-plan`
- `shepherd-task-20-create-issues-from-plan`
- `shepherd-task-30-from-assignment-to-ready`
- `shepherd-task-40-from-ready-to-merged-to-base`
- `shepherd-task-50-create-post-mortem`
- `shepherd-task-approve-workflows-and-wait-for-completion`

The scripts are shipped under `plugins/shepherd-task/scripts/`—currently 16 Bash/PowerShell pairs—but are not individually enumerated in `plugin.json`. The installer contains separate `SKILLS` and `REQUIRED_SCRIPTS` arrays, while the README’s File map documents the broader script inventory.

Thus, a precise shorthand is:

The shepherd-task estate consists of the skills declared by `plugins/shepherd-task/plugin.json` plus the runtime and support scripts under `plugins/shepherd-task/scripts/`.

`plugin.json` is authoritative for the skill lineup, but there is currently no single authoritative manifest enumerating the entire estate.

### 18:28 Prompt

But how does the `awesome-copilot-01/plugins/shepherd-task/version.{sh,ps1}` do its work without a manifest? Is the manifest effectively hard-coded in the script?

### 18:30 Response


For `version.{sh,ps1}`, the skill lineup is not hard-coded—it is read dynamically from `plugin.json`. During a version mutation, `Assert-SourceCheckout` iterates:

extensions.com.github.awesome-copilot.skills

and verifies that each referenced `SKILL.md` exists and is tracked by Git.

However, the script lineup is not manifested or validated individually. The version scripts treat everything under `plugins/shepherd-task/` as implicitly belonging to the version stored in `plugin.json`. They do not enumerate, hash, or even verify the files under `scripts/`.

There is some hard-coding elsewhere:

- `scripts/read-shepherd-task-version.{sh,ps1}` hard-codes the expected six skills when validating an installed plugin.
- `scripts/install-task-shepherd.{sh,ps1}` hard-codes both the six skills and a `REQUIRED_SCRIPTS` subset.
- `shepherd-task-version-contract.json` manifests protocol and artifact-schema versions, not estate membership.

So the current model is effectively:

```
┌──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Estate component         │ Source of membership                                                 │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Skills                   │ plugin.json                                                          │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Scripts                  │ Implicitly everything shipped beneath plugins/shepherd-task/scripts/ │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Required installer files │ Partially hard-coded in the installers                               │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Installed skill lineup   │ Hard-coded in read-shepherd-task-version.*                           │
└──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Thus, there is no complete shepherd-task estate manifest today. `plugin.json` is the lineup manifest for skills and the version authority for the whole plugin directory, but script membership is directory-convention-based. A script can be added, removed, or changed without `version.*` detecting that the lineup version should change.

Read validate-plugins.mjs L110:180 (70 lines read)
Read validate-plugins.mjs L300:405 (105 lines read)

Search "copy|cpSync|skills|hooks|agents|extensions" (eng/materialize-plugins.mjs) 44 lines found

Read plugin.json 34 lines read

Thought for 1s

Read materialize-plugins.mjs L100:180 (81 lines read)
Read materialize-plugins.mjs L70:110 (41 lines read)

Thought for 8s

There is a complete-set concept at the component/directory level, but not a universal file-level manifest.

For plugins, the source `plugin.json` declares the top-level reusable components under:

"extensions": {
  "com.github.awesome-copilot": {
    "agents": [],
    "hooks": [],
    "skills": [],
    "extensions": []
  }
}

Materialization then copies each referenced component:

- Agent: the referenced `.agent.md` file.
- Skill: the entire referenced skill directory, recursively—including bundled scripts, templates, and data.
- Hook: the entire referenced hook directory, recursively—including `hooks.json` and helper scripts.
- Canvas extension: the entire referenced extension directory, recursively.
- MCP configuration: separately declared by a plugin-root `mcp.json`.

Thus, for a conventional plugin, the complete content is determined transitively:

`plugin.json` component references + every file contained within each referenced component directory + plugin-root standard files.

What is missing is a standard field such as:

"scripts": [
  "./scripts/foo.sh",
  "./scripts/foo.ps1"
]

There is no supported `scripts` composition field, nor a generated lockfile listing every shipped file. Scripts normally belong inside a declared skill, hook, or extension, where directory membership makes them part of that component.

`shepherd-task` is unusual because it maintains a substantial plugin-root `scripts/` directory. Those scripts are included by the physical plugin-directory boundary, not by the declarative composition model. Therefore:

- Skills generally have a complete-set boundary: everything in the skill directory.
- Most plugins have a declarative component lineup: `plugin.json`.
- No plugin has a standard flat manifest of every constituent file.
- Plugin-root utility scripts like shepherd-task’s are not explicitly represented in `plugin.json`.

So `shepherd-task` exposes a real modeling gap: its logical estate is larger than the lineup described by its manifest. A dedicated estate manifest—or moving runtime scripts into a declared skill—would make that membership explicit and machine-verifiable.

Thought for 8s

No direct precedent exists in this repository. `shepherd-task` is currently the only plugin with a plugin-root `scripts/` directory or plugin-root Bash/PowerShell orchestration files. Other plugins do not appear to maintain executables outside declared components.

The closest precedents are script-heavy skills, such as `skills/copilot-pr-autopilot/`, which contains several coordinated PowerShell scripts. But those scripts are all bundled within—and owned by—one skill directory. Their membership is therefore unambiguous: declaring the skill includes its entire directory.

`shepherd-task` has a materially different architecture:

plugin
├── scripts/       # Cross-stage control plane
└── skills         # Stage-specific agent procedures

Its scripts:

- initiate and coordinate lifecycle stages;
- invoke multiple skills;
- validate outcomes shared between skills;
- maintain campaign-level state;
- install and version the combined lineup.

They are therefore plugin-level orchestration, not appropriate assets of any single skill. The repository’s standard composition model has no first-class representation for that pattern. The scripts are included merely because they physically reside in `plugins/shepherd-task/`.

So `shepherd-task` is both:

1. the only current example of this pattern; and
2. evidence that a concept such as plugin-level scripts, plugin runtime, or a shepherd-task estate manifest may be needed.

A suitable model could be a repository-specific composition field such as:

"extensions": {
  "com.github.awesome-copilot": {
    "skills": [
      "./skills/shepherd-task-10-create-ignorance-reduction-plan/",
      "./skills/shepherd-task-20-create-issues-from-plan/"
    ],
    "runtime": [
      "./scripts/"
    ]
  }
}

`runtime` or `pluginFiles` would be more accurate than `scripts`, because the estate also includes version contracts and possibly other non-executable runtime resources. The official Agent Plugins schema does not currently provide such a field, so this would need to remain in the repository-specific `com.github.awesome-copilot` build namespace and be materialized similarly to skills and hooks.

### 18:42 Prompt

Considering the "plugin" concept as manifested in this repository, is it an abuse of the concept to call `shepherd-task` a plugin?

### 18:43 Response

No—calling `shepherd-task` a plugin is not an abuse of the repository’s concept, but it is an architectural outlier.

It satisfies the repository’s essential plugin contract:

- It has `plugins/shepherd-task/plugin.json` and `README.md`.
- Its manifest declares a valid name, version, schema, and skill composition.
- It is installed and distributed as one coherent capability.
- Its constituent skills serve a common workflow.
- Its plugin directory supplies shared implementation needed by that workflow.

What makes it unusual is that most plugins here are primarily curated bundles of independently usable components:

plugin = metadata + references to agents/skills/hooks/extensions

`shepherd-task` is closer to an integrated application or workflow system:

plugin = control plane scripts + shared contracts + stage-specific skills

The scripts own sequencing and state, while the skills provide agent-driven stages. Neither side alone constitutes the product. That is still a legitimate plugin architecture: a plugin can be a deployment and versioning boundary, not merely a list of loosely related skills.

The mismatch lies in the repository’s composition tooling, not in the word “plugin.” The tooling can explicitly describe the skills but cannot explicitly describe the plugin-level control plane. Consequently, `shepherd-task` is a valid plugin whose complete composition is only partially represented by `plugin.json`.

I would characterize it as:

`shepherd-task` is an integrated orchestration plugin, rather than a conventional collection plugin. Its plugin-level scripts form a shared control plane that coordinates its stage-specific skills.

So it stretches the repository’s current plugin model, but does not misuse it. The repository model should likely be expanded to represent integrated plugins like this one explicitly.No—calling `shepherd-task` a plugin is not an abuse of the repository’s concept, but it is an architectural outlier.

### 18:43 Prompt

But "plugin", in this context, is a well-defined term, right? I mean there is this "agent plugins" spec, right?

### 18:45 Response

Thought for 20s

Searched the web "site:agent-plugins.org Agent Plugins specification plugin manifest bundled files skills scripts"

Thought for 9s

Yes. “Agent Plugin” is a formally defined term under the Agent Plugins Specification v1.0.0. An Agent Plugin is a self-contained directory with a root `plugin.json`; portable components are discovered from fixed locations rather than enumerated in the manifest. Version 1 defines exactly two portable component types: skills under `skills/*/SKILL.md` and MCP servers configured by root `mcp.json`. (agent-plugins.org)

This sharpens—and partially corrects—my earlier characterization:

- `plugin.json` is primarily the plugin’s identity and metadata manifest.
- Under the portable spec, it does not list the complete contents or enumerate skills.
- Skills are discovered by scanning the plugin’s `skills/` directory.
- Files such as scripts may exist inside the package, but they are not independently recognized as Agent Plugin components.
- Scripts bundled inside a skill belong to that skill under the Agent Skills model. (agent-plugins.org)

The `extensions.com.github.awesome-copilot` section in this repository’s source manifests is repository-specific build metadata. It tells this repository how to assemble shared source resources into a self-contained plugin directory. It is not the portable Agent Plugins mechanism for enumerating components; the repository removes that composition metadata from the served manifest. The specification expressly permits namespaced client-specific data, but assigns it no portable semantics. (agent-plugins.org)

Consequently, `shepherd-task` is validly an Agent Plugin as a package: it has the required root manifest and contains discoverable skills. Its plugin-root scripts are also valid package files. But, in strict specification terminology, those scripts are not Agent Plugin components. Agent Plugins v1 has no portable “plugin-level scripts” component type and gives those files no automatic execution or discovery semantics. (agent-plugins.org)

The precise characterization is therefore:

`shepherd-task` is an Agent Plugin containing Agent Skills, plus a non-portable plugin-level orchestration runtime implemented as package-local scripts.

GitHub Copilot itself supports additional components—such as agents, hooks, and client integrations—additively around the portable Agent Plugins format. That makes the broader package legitimate for Copilot, but the script-driven control plane remains a Copilot/repository convention rather than something defined by Agent Plugins v1. (docs.github.com)

### 18:47 Prompt

Is this a gap in the Agent Plugins specification?

### 18:47 Response


Yes, but narrowly: it is a gap if the desired capability is portable, plugin-level executable orchestration. It is not necessarily a defect in the specification’s package model.

Agent Plugins v1.0.0 deliberately defines a minimal interoperability floor: skills and MCP servers. It defines the plugin as the entire self-contained directory, so arbitrary files can be packaged, but it assigns no portable discovery or execution semantics to plugin-root scripts. (github.com)

There are two distinct concerns:

┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Concern                                           │ Gap in Agent Plugins v1?                                                │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Include arbitrary scripts in the plugin package   │ No. They are files within the plugin root.                              │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Enumerate every packaged file                     │ No explicit support, but this is primarily packaging/integrity tooling. │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Declare scripts as named commands or entry points │ Yes. No portable component type exists for this.                        │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Define when and how those commands execute        │ Yes. Only MCP server subprocess execution is standardized.              │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Allow a particular client to implement this       │ No. Client extensions are the intended escape hatch.                    │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The specification explicitly supports namespaced client extensions for capabilities outside the portable core. A client may define additional manifest data, directories, validation rules, and behavior; other clients may ignore them while the plugin remains conformant. (agent-plugins.org)

Therefore, `shepherd-task` exposes this particular missing abstraction:

A plugin-level command or workflow runtime that coordinates multiple skills, maintains shared state, and exposes executable lifecycle entry points without modeling those entry points as MCP tools.

Whether that should enter the portable specification depends on portability:

- If shepherd-task’s orchestration is inherently GitHub Copilot-specific, a `com.github.copilot` client extension is the correct solution.
- If several agent clients need to install and invoke the same orchestration commands consistently, then it is evidence for a new portable component type—perhaps `commands`, `workflows`, or `runtimes`.

The specification describes itself as a deliberately small interoperability floor, and its public process expects proposed additions to demonstrate a concrete portability requirement and implementer support. There is already public interest in a roadmap beyond skills and MCP, reinforcing that this boundary is recognized, though not yet standardized. (github.com)

So the most accurate conclusion is:

Shepherd-task reveals a deliberate extensibility boundary that becomes a specification gap when cross-client portability of plugin-level orchestration is required.

An estate inventory alone probably does not belong in the core specification. A standardized executable-command component—with entry points, arguments, environment, permissions, lifecycle, and failure semantics—would be the substantive specification proposal.
Yes, but narrowly: it is a gap if the desired capability is portable, plugin-level executable orchestration. It is not necessarily a defect in the specification’s package model.

Agent Plugins v1.0.0 deliberately defines a minimal interoperability floor: skills and MCP servers. It defines the plugin as the entire self-contained directory, so arbitrary files can be packaged, but it assigns no portable discovery or execution semantics to plugin-root scripts. (github.com)

There are two distinct concerns:

┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Concern                                           │ Gap in Agent Plugins v1?                                                │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Include arbitrary scripts in the plugin package   │ No. They are files within the plugin root.                              │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Enumerate every packaged file                     │ No explicit support, but this is primarily packaging/integrity tooling. │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Declare scripts as named commands or entry points │ Yes. No portable component type exists for this.                        │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Define when and how those commands execute        │ Yes. Only MCP server subprocess execution is standardized.              │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Allow a particular client to implement this       │ No. Client extensions are the intended escape hatch.                    │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The specification explicitly supports namespaced client extensions for capabilities outside the portable core. A client may define additional manifest data, directories, validation rules, and behavior; other clients may ignore them while the plugin remains conformant. (agent-plugins.org)

Therefore, `shepherd-task` exposes this particular missing abstraction:

A plugin-level command or workflow runtime that coordinates multiple skills, maintains shared state, and exposes executable lifecycle entry points without modeling those entry points as MCP tools.

Whether that should enter the portable specification depends on portability:

- If shepherd-task’s orchestration is inherently GitHub Copilot-specific, a `com.github.copilot` client extension is the correct solution.
- If several agent clients need to install and invoke the same orchestration commands consistently, then it is evidence for a new portable component type—perhaps `commands`, `workflows`, or `runtimes`.

The specification describes itself as a deliberately small interoperability floor, and its public process expects proposed additions to demonstrate a concrete portability requirement and implementer support. There is already public interest in a roadmap beyond skills and MCP, reinforcing that this boundary is recognized, though not yet standardized. (github.com)

So the most accurate conclusion is:

Shepherd-task reveals a deliberate extensibility boundary that becomes a specification gap when cross-client portability of plugin-level orchestration is required.

An estate inventory alone probably does not belong in the core specification. A standardized executable-command component—with entry points, arguments, environment, permissions, lifecycle, and failure semantics—would be the substantive specification proposal.
Yes, but narrowly: it is a gap if the desired capability is portable, plugin-level executable orchestration. It is not necessarily a defect in the specification’s package model.

Agent Plugins v1.0.0 deliberately defines a minimal interoperability floor: skills and MCP servers. It defines the plugin as the entire self-contained directory, so arbitrary files can be packaged, but it assigns no portable discovery or execution semantics to plugin-root scripts. (github.com)

There are two distinct concerns:

```
┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Concern                                           │ Gap in Agent Plugins v1?                                                │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Include arbitrary scripts in the plugin package   │ No. They are files within the plugin root.                              │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Enumerate every packaged file                     │ No explicit support, but this is primarily packaging/integrity tooling. │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Declare scripts as named commands or entry points │ Yes. No portable component type exists for this.                        │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Define when and how those commands execute        │ Yes. Only MCP server subprocess execution is standardized.              │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤

└───────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Therefore, `shepherd-task` exposes this particular missing abstraction:

Whether that should enter the portable specification depends on portability:

The specification describes itself as a deliberately small interoperability floor, and its public process expects proposed additions to demonstrate a concrete portability requirement and implementer support. There is already public interest in a roadmap beyond skills and MCP, reinforcing that this boundary is recognized, though not yet standardized. (github.com)

>   "./version.ps1",
>   "./version.sh"
> ]
>

- Bash: `rm -rf "$PLUGIN_DIR"`
- PowerShell: `Remove-Item $pluginDir -Recurse -Force`

However, `test/` is not declared in `pluginFiles`. This means the current installer behavior includes files beyond the newly declared estate. If `pluginFiles` is intended to be authoritative, either:

No—the `run-campaign.ps1` scripts are explicitly designed to run from the installed plugin. They do not derive resources from their own checked-out repository location. Instead, both calculate:

$ShepherdPlugin = Join-Path $CopilotHome 'plugins\shepherd-task'
$FixtureRoot = Join-Path $ShepherdPlugin 'test\<fixture>'

They even fail with messages such as:

Installed simple-math fixture was not found...
Install shepherd-task before running this driver.

The invoked scripts are also compatible with the installed layout:

- `..\..\scripts` resolves to the installed plugin’s `scripts/`.
- `..\..\..\..\skills` resolves to `$COPILOT_HOME/skills/`.
- `..\..\..\..\plugins\shepherd-task` resolves back to the installed plugin.
- Fixture scripts using `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` are invoked only after the driver changes directory to the cloned target or control worktree.

Therefore, the expected topology is:

$COPILOT_HOME/
├── plugins/
│   └── shepherd-task/
│       ├── scripts/
│       └── test/
│           ├── simple-math/
│           │   └── run-campaign.ps1
│           └── cargotracker-add-change-arrival-deadline-feature/
│               └── run-campaign.ps1
└── skills/
    └── shepherd-task-*/

The real inconsistency is not runtime compatibility. It is that `test/` is required runtime content for these campaign drivers, yet `pluginFiles` currently omits it. If installation is later changed to copy only declared `pluginFiles`, both campaign drivers and their fixture/contract scripts will disappear or fail. If these drivers are intended to remain supported, `./test/` should be added to `pluginFiles` and its executable scripts included in estate versioning.Thought for 1s

My assertion was materially false as stated. I said the campaigns could be run from the installed location, but the first real installed simple-math campaign failed before Stage 20 because of a path-comparison bug. “Can be run” reasonably means the campaign progresses successfully, not merely that the files are installed and the driver starts.

The change is evidence of the following:

• False: The existing offline and installation checks were sufficient evidence that the campaign was runnable.
• True but narrower: The installer copied the required files, the driver found the installed fixture and shared scripts,  --help  worked outside the repository, and no source-checkout path was required.
• Not demonstrated: That the installed location itself caused the bug. The same lexical-versus-canonical path comparison could also fail from a source checkout. The defect was path handling, not an unavoidable dependency on repository layout.

My validation only tested file presence, executability, cwd-independent help, and isolated offline contracts. It did not exercise the integration boundary where  02-create-issues.sh  compares its expected helper paths with paths emitted by Stage 15. I should have said:

The drivers appear location-independent and their non-paid checks pass from an installed copy, but a real end-to-end campaign has not been run.

Todo completed analyze-simple-math-ci-failure

The CI did not fail. The outer Stage 25 orchestrator incorrectly interpreted an unsupported GitHub CLI option as failed CI.

After Stage 30 successfully completed, `scripts/shepherd-task.sh` executed:

gh pr checks "$pr_number" -R "$REPO" --json name,state,bucket ...

Your installed GitHub CLI is `gh 2.45.0`, whose `gh pr checks` command does not support `--json`. It exits nonzero with:

unknown flag: --json

Because `ci_passing()` redirects stderr to `/dev/null` and converts any command failure into a false result, the real error was hidden and replaced with:

FAILED: CI checks not passing on PR #5 after Phase 1.

The two checks for PR #5 at HEAD `3bae383564a8fd2e88f732f9060f32d408e62a4f` both actually completed successfully. Stage 30 independently verified this through the GitHub API and correctly emitted `SHEPHERD COMPLETE`.

The failure sequence was therefore:

1. Stage 30 completed successfully at approximately `23:17:58 UTC`.
2. The outer script found PR #5 and validated its state.
3. `gh pr checks --json` failed because the option is unsupported.
4. The outer script mislabeled that command failure as failed CI.
5. Stage 40 was never launched.
6. Stage 50 generated the post-mortem, accounting for most of the interval before the outer run ended at `23:19:09 UTC`.

The same incompatible command appears in five relevant places:

- `scripts/shepherd-task.sh`
- `scripts/shepherd-task.ps1`
- `scripts/shepherd-task-monitor.sh`
- `scripts/shepherd-task-monitor.ps1`
- `skills/shepherd-task-30-from-assignment-to-ready/SKILL.md`

Also, the run used installed shepherd-task `1.0.1`. However, the same defect remains in the current `1.0.2` source, so reinstalling alone would not fix it.

No files were changed, and the version was not incremented.

### 23:37 Prompt

How can I discern the current operating `gh` version on GNU/Linux and Windows?

### 23:38 Response

!Thought for 2s
I'm looking at some simple CLI commands for checking the version of GitHub's CLI tool. I don’t need to go into documentation, as it's straightforward. For GNU/Linux, I can use `command -v gh` or `gh --version`, and for Windows, I'd try `Get-Command gh` in PowerShell or `where gh` in cmd. I might also want to mention the possibility of multiple versions depending on the path configuration.

Use these commands to identify both the executable being invoked and its version.

GNU/Linux:

command -v gh
gh --version

To list every `gh` found on `PATH`:

type -a gh

Windows PowerShell:

(Get-Command gh).Source
gh --version

To find every matching executable:

Get-Command gh -All | Select-Object -ExpandProperty Source

Windows Command Prompt:

where gh
gh --version

The first path reported is normally the executable currently selected by `PATH`.!Thought for 2s

### 23:39 Prompt

How do I upgrade `gh` on Windows, macOS and GNU/Linux?

### 23:41 Response

!Thought for 27s
I need to consider that package manager instructions can frequently change, so it's crucial to look this up online. The user is asking for recommendations on updating GitHub, so I'll focus on searching the official GitHub documentation first, as that's the best source. I should make sure to cite all my claims if they're coming from the internet. I might also use a web search for the official installation documentation for the CLI.

Searched the web "site:cli.github.com manual installation upgrade GitHub CLI Windows macOS Linux"

Thought for 19s
I need to find the official Windows installation information. I think it's a good idea to check the official README for methods on installing Windows, possibly using tools like Winget, Chocolatey (choco), or Scoop. These tools can streamline the installation process, so getting the correct official sources will help ensure I have accurate and reliable information. Let's explore this together and see what I can uncover!

Searched the web "site:github.com/cli/cli Windows install upgrade winget GitHub CLI official"

Searched the web "site:cli.github.com/manual/gh_pr_checks --json GitHub CLI"

Upgrade `gh` using the same package manager that originally installed it; this avoids multiple installations on `PATH`.

Windows — WinGet, recommended:

winget upgrade --id GitHub.cli --source winget

Open a new Windows Terminal window afterward so PATH changes take effect. If installed using another manager:

choco upgrade gh
# or
scoop update gh

WinGet is GitHub CLI’s officially recommended Windows method; Chocolatey and Scoop packages are community-maintained. (github.com)

macOS — Homebrew:

brew update
brew upgrade gh

If Homebrew says `gh` is not installed:

brew install gh

Homebrew is the recommended package-manager installation on macOS. (github.com)

Ubuntu/Debian: Because Ubuntu’s community package can remain substantially behind, GitHub currently recommends its official package repository. Your Ubuntu-provided `2.45.0` installation is an example where simply running `apt upgrade` may not obtain a sufficiently recent release. (github.com)

Configure the official repository once:

sudo mkdir -p -m 755 /etc/apt/keyrings

wget -qO- https://cli.github.com/packages/githubcli-archive-keyring.gpg |
  sudo tee /etc/apt/keyrings/githubcli-archive-keyring.gpg >/dev/null

sudo chmod go+r /etc/apt/keyrings/githubcli-archive-keyring.gpg

echo "deb [arch=$(dpkg --print-architecture) signed-by=/etc/apt/keyrings/githubcli-archive-keyring.gpg] https://cli.github.com/packages stable main" |
  sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/github-cli.list >/dev/null

sudo apt update
sudo apt install gh

Subsequent upgrades are:

sudo apt update
sudo apt install gh

Fedora/RHEL with the official GitHub CLI RPM repository:

sudo dnf update gh

Arch Linux:

sudo pacman -Syu

The official Linux documentation also provides commands for DNF4, DNF5, yum, zypper, Alpine, and other distributions. (github.com)

Finally, verify the selected executable and required capability:

command -v gh
gh --version
gh pr checks --help | grep -- --json

On Windows PowerShell:

(Get-Command gh).Source
gh --version
gh pr checks --help | Select-String -- '--json'

Current GitHub CLI documentation includes `--json` support for `gh pr checks`. (cli.github.com)!Thought for 27s

Upgrade `gh` using the same package manager that originally installed it; this avoids multiple installations on `PATH`.

Windows — WinGet, recommended:

winget upgrade --id GitHub.cli --source winget

Open a new Windows Terminal window afterward so PATH changes take effect. If installed using another manager:

choco upgrade gh
# or
scoop update gh

WinGet is GitHub CLI’s officially recommended Windows method; Chocolatey and Scoop packages are community-maintained. (github.com)

macOS — Homebrew:

brew update
brew upgrade gh

If Homebrew says `gh` is not installed:

brew install gh

Homebrew is the recommended package-manager installation on macOS. (github.com)

Ubuntu/Debian: Because Ubuntu’s community package can remain substantially behind, GitHub currently recommends its official package repository. Your Ubuntu-provided `2.45.0` installation is an example where simply running `apt upgrade` may not obtain a sufficiently recent release. (github.com)

Configure the official repository once:

sudo mkdir -p -m 755 /etc/apt/keyrings

wget -qO- https://cli.github.com/packages/githubcli-archive-keyring.gpg |
  sudo tee /etc/apt/keyrings/githubcli-archive-keyring.gpg >/dev/null

sudo chmod go+r /etc/apt/keyrings/githubcli-archive-keyring.gpg

echo "deb [arch=$(dpkg --print-architecture) signed-by=/etc/apt/keyrings/githubcli-archive-keyring.gpg] https://cli.github.com/packages stable main" |
  sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/github-cli.list >/dev/null

sudo apt update
sudo apt install gh

Subsequent upgrades are:

sudo apt update
sudo apt install gh

Fedora/RHEL with the official GitHub CLI RPM repository:

sudo dnf update gh

Arch Linux:

sudo pacman -Syu

The official Linux documentation also provides commands for DNF4, DNF5, yum, zypper, Alpine, and other distributions. (github.com)

Finally, verify the selected executable and required capability:

command -v gh
gh --version
gh pr checks --help | grep -- --json

On Windows PowerShell:

(Get-Command gh).Source
gh --version
gh pr checks --help | Select-String -- '--json'

Current GitHub CLI documentation includes `--json` support for `gh pr checks`. (cli.github.com)

For your Ubuntu GNU/Linux machine, Microsoft’s preferred installation method is its official package repository:

sudo apt-get update
sudo apt-get install -y wget apt-transport-https software-properties-common

source /etc/os-release
wget -q "https://packages.microsoft.com/config/ubuntu/$VERSION_ID/packages-microsoft-prod.deb"

sudo dpkg -i packages-microsoft-prod.deb
rm packages-microsoft-prod.deb

sudo apt-get install -y powershell

Microsoft documents installation from `packages.microsoft.com` as the preferred Ubuntu method. (learn.microsoft.com)

Or run an individual script:

pwsh -NoLogo -NoProfile -File ./math-tool.ps1 -N 10

Expected output for that example:

Fibonacci(10) = 55

macOS: Microsoft’s official distribution is the signed and notarized `.pkg` installer, with separate Arm64 and x64 packages. As of September 10, 2026, the documented LTS release is PowerShell 7.6.6. Download the package matching the Mac’s architecture and open it in Finder. (learn.microsoft.com)

Determine the Mac architecture with:

uname -m

- `arm64`: Apple Silicon package
- `x86_64`: Intel package

Windows clients: Microsoft recommends WinGet:

winget install --id Microsoft.PowerShell --source winget

PowerShell 7 installs as `pwsh` alongside Windows PowerShell 5.1 rather than replacing it. (learn.microsoft.com)

The checkout confirms this was not stale state:  gh repo clone  created  origin  for your fork and  upstream  for its parent. I’m checking the resolver contract to determine whether the driver’s “exactly one remote” guard is redundant or intentionally stricter than the rest of shepherd-task.

Shell Inspect clone-related configuration 14 lines…
git config --global --get-regexp '^(clone|remote)\.' || true; gh config list

Task complete 
This failed because the repository is a fork, not because cleanup was incomplete.  gh repo clone  created two remotes by design:

 -  origin  →  edburns/dd-3061974-01-cargotracker 
 -  upstream  →  azure-javaee/cargotracker 

The driver then incorrectly requires the clone to have exactly one remote, so it stopped with “found 2.” Its own  resolve-repository-remote.sh  already supports multiple remotes and can uniquely select  origin , making that guard redundant.

The script reached cloning only after confirming both workarea paths did not exist, so pre-run cleanup was sufficient. This failed run did leave  /Users/edburns/workareas/dd-3061974-01-cargotracker-shepherd-target  behind; remove that specific checkout before retrying. It failed before publishing the baseline or creating the control worktree/branches.

Search "\$remotes\[0\]|\$Remotes\[0\]|\$resolvedRemote|\$ResolvedRemote" in *.ps1 (plugins/shepherd-task/test) 14 lines found
Search "\$\{remotes\[0\]\}|resolved_remote" in run-campaign.sh (plugins/shepherd-task/test) 7 lines found

The driver should allow multiple remotes and select the one matching the requested repository. The shared resolver already does this correctly.

Recommended fix

In each campaign driver:

1. Remove the check requiring  git remote  to return exactly one remote.
2. Call  resolve-repository-remote.sh  or  .ps1 .
3. Use the returned remote for branch checks instead of hardcoding the first remote.

For Bash, change:

git -C "$target" ls-remote --exit-code --heads "${remotes[0]}" "$branch"

to:

git -C "$target" ls-remote --exit-code --heads "$resolved_remote" "$branch"

For PowerShell, change:

git -C $Target ls-remote --exit-code --heads $remotes[0] $branch

to:

git -C $Target ls-remote --exit-code --heads $resolvedRemote $branch

The resolver will select  origin  because it matches  edburns/dd-3061974-01-cargotracker ; the unrelated  upstream  remote will be safely ignored.

Windows impact

Yes, Windows is affected. This is GitHub CLI behavior, not a macOS behavior:  gh repo clone  adds an  upstream  remote when cloning a fork on every supported platform. Therefore, the active PowerShell drivers need the same correction:

•  test/cargotracker-add-change-arrival-deadline-feature/run-campaign.ps1 
•  test/simple-math/run-campaign.ps1 

The corresponding Bash drivers need correction too, and the historical treatment/control PowerShell drivers contain the same assumption.

Tests should also add a fork-shaped case: one matching  origin  plus one nonmatching  upstream , expecting the resolver to return  origin .

Using  gh repo clone --no-upstream  would be a smaller workaround, but changing the drivers to use their existing resolver is more robust and avoids rejecting legitimate extra remotes. Before testing the eventual fix, remove the failed  ...-shepherd-target  checkout and reinstall the updated plugin.

Search "set \+e[\s\S]{0,180}ls-remote|ls-remote --exit-code --heads" in *.sh (plugins/shepherd-task/test) 13 lines found
Search "trap on_error ERR|trap .* ERR|set \+e" (plugins/shepherd-task/test/cargotracker-add-change-arrival-deadline-feature/r… 8 lines found

Shell Confirm expected absent branch exit 2 lines…
target=/Users/edburns/workareas/dd-3061974-01-cargotracker-shepherd-target; cd "$target"; remote=$(/Users/edburns/.copilot/plugins/shepherd…

This was a Bash  ERR  trap bug, not incomplete cleanup.

After cloning, the driver checks whether the baseline branch already exists:

set +e
git ls-remote --exit-code --heads "$resolved_remote" "$branch"
branch_exit=$?
set -e

The branch does not exist, so  git ls-remote --exit-code  correctly returns 2. However,  set +e  disables automatic exit but does not disable an active  ERR  trap. The global  on_error  trap fired immediately and exited before  branch_exit=$?  could handle the expected result. That explains:

• Why the failure was still labeled “cloning primary checkout.”
• Why no specific  Error:  message appeared.
• Why the preserved clone is complete and clean.
• Why both experiment branches remain absent.

The Cargo Tracker Bash driver should use the same safe conditional structure already used by the simple-math driver:

if git -C "$target" ls-remote --exit-code --heads \
    "$resolved_remote" "$branch" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
    branch_exit=0
else
    branch_exit=$?
fi

Commands used as an  if  condition do not trigger the  ERR  trap. This particular failure affects only the Bash Cargo Tracker driver; the PowerShell driver’s  $LASTEXITCODE  handling is not subject to Bash  ERR  traps.

The failed run again left  dd-3061974-01-cargotracker-shepherd-target  behind, so that checkout must be removed before another attempt.

The intended Stage 10 invocation is conversational, not a PowerShell script. In a real campaign it runs after Stage 00 initializes the campaign directory and before Stage 15 prepares issue creation.

1. Start Copilot CLI from the target repository on the campaign base branch:

copilot

2. Confirm/select the installed skill:

/skills

Select:

shepherd-task-10-create-ignorance-reduction-plan

3. Load the relevant context using `@` for files and `#` for GitHub issues/PRs, then give the skill its two conceptual inputs. For example:

Use the shepherd-task-10-create-ignorance-reduction-plan skill.

FILENAME:
123-arrival-deadline-remove-before-merge/arrival-deadline-ignorance-reduction-plan.md

CONTEXT:
Create the campaign plan for #123.

Use these materials:
@docs/adr/arrival-deadline.md
@src/main/java/example/Cargo.java
@123-arrival-deadline-remove-before-merge/shepherd-campaign.json

Identify every implementation-gating unknown and any necessary spikes.
Leave every Resolution section empty.
Create ordered, separately testable implementation tasks with files,
tests, and gating criteria. Do not implement production code or create
GitHub issues.

You can also explicitly name the skill in the prompt without selecting it first; Copilot should infer and load it. Selecting it through `/skills` makes the intent unambiguous.

The key contract is:

- `FILENAME` must be inside the Stage 00 campaign directory and end with `ignorance-reduction-plan.md`.
- `CONTEXT` must already be available in the conversation. The skill synthesizes context; it is not responsible for researching or discovering all context itself.
- The skill writes the plan with empty `Resolution:` blocks.
- Human/Copilot research and spikes happen next, filling every implementation-gating resolution.
- Once resolved, commit and push the plan, then run Stage 15:

.\plugins\shepherd-task\scripts\shepherd-task-15-prepare-create-issues.ps1 `
  -CampaignMetadataDirectory 123-arrival-deadline-remove-before-merge

The simple-math and Cargo Tracker drivers deliberately replace both Stage 10 and the human research gate with embedded, already-resolved fixture plans, which is why you have not been exercising this invocation.

The driver does contain an “Actual invocation,” but it prints too late.

The sequence in `run-campaign.ps1` is:

1. Print the planned Stage 15 invocation.
2. Print the planned Stage 25 invocation.
3. Run `02-create-issues.ps1`.
4. Only after that script succeeds, print the Stage 15 “Actual invocation.”

Inside `02-create-issues.ps1`, the actual work already happens:

$artifacts = & $preparationScript `
    -CampaignMetadataDirectory $CampaignMetadataDirectory `
    -PassThru

It then executes the generated Stage 20 launcher and verifies the created issues. Therefore, while Stage 15 or Stage 20 is running, you see no corresponding actual line. If either fails—or the run is still waiting for Stage 20—the driver never reaches the “Actual invocation” output.

So this is an instrumentation-ordering defect. The actual invocation is currently a retrospective success message, not a declaration immediately before execution. It should instead be printed inside `02-create-issues.ps1` immediately before calling `$preparationScript`, or the driver should directly invoke Stage 15 and pass its artifacts into the fixture’s Stage 20 logic. ### 03:53 Response

Yes, but not by merely moving the existing log statement earlier in `run-campaign.ps1`. That would claim Stage 15 was invoked before the wrapper actually reaches it, and the wrapper can fail during its preliminary validation.

Two valid approaches exist:

1. Small, accurate fix: Move responsibility for the “Actual invocation” message into `02-create-issues.ps1`. Print it immediately before:
$artifacts = & $preparationScript `
    -CampaignMetadataDirectory $CampaignMetadataDirectory `
    -PassThru
Then remove the late message from `run-campaign.ps1`. This accurately reflects execution, but duplicates some invocation-formatting logic or requires extracting the formatter into a shared helper.
2. Cleaner architectural fix: Make `run-campaign.ps1` invoke Stage 15 directly:
- Print the actual invocation.
- Execute Stage 15 and capture its returned artifact paths.
- Pass those artifact paths to `02-create-issues.ps1`.
- Have `02-create-issues.ps1` validate/customize the generated prompt, execute Stage 20, and verify the issues without invoking Stage 15 again.

The second approach produces the clearest lifecycle:

Stage 15 heading
Actual Stage 15 invocation
Stage 15 execution
Stage 20 heading
Stage 20 launcher execution
Stage 20 verification

It requires a real interface change to `02-create-issues.ps1`, likely passing `PromptFile`, `InvocationFile`, and `ArtifactDirectory` or a single Stage 15 artifact directory. The same refactoring should be applied to both simple-math and Cargo Tracker to keep their fixture drivers aligned.

So it is feasible. ### 04:04 Response

!Thought for 4s
I can explain based on the code that I've already seen. First, I need to discuss how the current directory is inherited via Set-Location. It might help to specify exact locations and review Set-Location before the wrapper and script that uses git rev-parse. It's important to note that parent processes change the current working directory (cwd), and children inherit this. I should be concise since the user is asking for a conceptual understanding based on the inspected code. There could be hidden states and ambiguity to mention as well.

It does not derive the control-worktree path from `-CampaignMetadataDirectory`. It relies on the process’s current working directory.

Earlier, `run-campaign.ps1` executes:

Set-Location -LiteralPath $ControlWorktree

It later launches `02-create-issues.ps1` using `pwsh`. That child process inherits the control worktree as its working directory. `02-create-issues.ps1` invokes Stage 15 in-process, preserving that directory.

Stage 15 discovers the repository root with:

$repoRootOutput = git rev-parse --show-toplevel
$repoRoot = [System.IO.Path]::GetFullPath(
    ($repoRootOutput | Select-Object -First 1).Trim()
)

Because Git is running from:

C:\Users\edburns\workareas\dd-3056167-01-windows-shepherd-control

it returns that fully qualified path. Stage 15 then resolves the relative metadata directory beneath it:

$campaignMetadataPath = [System.IO.Path]::GetFullPath(
    Join-Path $repoRoot $CampaignMetadataDirectory
)

Thus, the effective invocation has an implicit input:

Current working directory:
C:\Users\edburns\workareas\dd-3056167-01-windows-shepherd-control

The displayed invocation is shell-correct only when run from inside the intended campaign worktree. This is also why Stage 15 documents: “Run this script inside the campaign Git worktree.” The behavior is valid, but the output would be clearer if it printed the working directory immediately before the invocation.

It failed in Stage 40 for issue #9 / PR #14, during the local GitHub CLI capability preflight—before the PR was marked ready, before Copilot review was requested, and before anything was merged.

Why it failed

The Stage 40 skill prescribed this Bash check under  set -euo pipefail :

gh pr edit --help | grep -Fq '@copilot'

This has a race-dependent false-failure mode:

1.  grep -q  exits immediately after finding the first match.
2.  gh  may still be writing help output and receive  SIGPIPE .
3. Because  pipefail  is enabled, the pipeline can return nonzero even though  grep  found  @copilot .
4. Stage 40 incorrectly reports that the installed CLI lacks support.

The evidence is strong:

• Your installed  /opt/homebrew/bin/gh  is version 2.100.0.
• It was installed at 5:01 PM PDT on September 9, before this campaign.
• Its help contains multiple documented references to  @copilot .
• Stage 40 for PR #13 used non-quiet  grep -F , found the same token, successfully requested Copilot review, and merged the PR.
• Stage 40 for PR #14 switched to  grep -Fq  and failed.
• The generated post-mortem independently identified this same  grep -q / pipefail  interaction.

This is not specifically a macOS incompatibility. It is a Bash pipeline bug that can occur on macOS or GNU/Linux depending on pipe-buffer size and process scheduling.

Fix plan

This is a defect in the core Stage 40 skill, not in the Cargo Tracker driver. Per your earlier instruction, I have stopped without modifying source.

The fix should:

1. Replace the piped  grep -q  preflight in  skills/shepherd-task-40-from-ready-to-merged-to-base/SKILL.md .
2. Capture the complete  gh pr edit --help  output and native exit status first.
3. Inspect the captured string for  @copilot , avoiding an early-closing pipeline.
4. Improve failure diagnostics with the resolved  gh  path and version.
5. Update the active and historical simple-math/Cargo Tracker contracts that currently require the unsafe pattern.
6. Add a regression using help output larger than a pipe buffer, proving it works under  set -euo pipefail .
7. Validate Bash and PowerShell contracts, plugin and skill validation, and the build while keeping version  1.0.2 .

The PowerShell implementation already captures the complete output before searching it, so its runtime logic does not have this particular bug. Its contracts should still be checked for consistency.

Resume rather than restart

Resuming is safer and substantially cheaper. Do not rerun  run-campaign.sh , and do not delete any issues, PRs, branches, worktrees, or artifacts.

After the Stage 40 fix is committed, pushed, and reinstalled, confirm the installed skill no longer contains the unsafe pipeline:

grep -n "gh pr edit --help" \
  ~/.copilot/plugins/shepherd-task/skills/shepherd-task-40-from-ready-to-merged-to-base/SKILL.md

Then start a new Stage 25 run for the failed and unstarted subset:

cd /Users/edburns/workareas/dd-3061974-01-cargotracker-shepherd-control

/Users/edburns/.copilot/plugins/shepherd-task/scripts/shepherd-task-25-given-list.sh \
  9,10,11,12 \
  7-arrival-deadline-control-remove-before-merge

This creates a new run directory. It should rediscover existing PR #14, revalidate its completed Stage 30 state, continue through Stage 40, and then process issues #10–#12. The prior failed run remains valid historical evidence.

A from-scratch restart is not cleaner here: PR #13 is already merged and issue #8 is closed, so fully erasing the run would require destructive history manipulation or recreating the disposable repository.
